K = int(input())
S = input().strip()
n = len(S)

# Compute inverse permutation
inv_perm = [0] * n
for i in range(n):
    if 2 * i < n:
        inv_perm[i] = 2 * i
    else:
        inv_perm[i] = 2 * (n - 1 - i) + 1

# Find cycles and compute result after K inverse swaps
visited = [False] * n
result = [''] * n

for i in range(n):
    if not visited[i]:
        cycle = []
        current = i
        while True:
            cycle.append(current)
            visited[current] = True
            current = inv_perm[current]
            if current == i:
                break
        cycle_len = len(cycle)
        for idx in range(cycle_len):
            pos = cycle[idx]
            steps = K % cycle_len
            final_pos = cycle[(idx + steps) % cycle_len]
            result[pos] = S[final_pos]

print(''.join(result))<#sep#>Please answer the given financial question based on the context.

Processing each edge once.

Now, we have to track degrees and s for each node.

The steps for implementation:

First, read all the data into arrays:

degrees array: an array of size n where degree[i] is the degree of node i.

s array: array s[i].

Then, create a queue (preferably implemented as a deque for efficient popping from front).

First, initialize the queue with all nodes with degree[i] ==1.

Wait but the problem is that once we start processing nodes, their neighbors might also end up with degrees 1, getting added to the queue.

Also, we need to represent the s and degrees as mutable arrays, since each time we process a node, we modify the degree and s of its neighbor.

degrees = [0]*n

from collections import deque

q = deque()

for i in range(n):

    if degrees[i] == 1:

        q.append(i)

edges = []

    v = q.popleft()

    if degrees[v] !=1: # possible, since other processes may have changed it?

        continue

    w = s_list[v] (the other end)

    # Add edge (v, w)

    edges.append( (v, w) )

    # Now, process for w.

    degrees[v] -=1 → but actually, since degree[v] was 1 before being popped, so set to 0.

    s_list[v] ^= w? → no, since v is now a leaf shed all edges.

    Wait not sure, but the w's side:

    degrees[w] -=1

    s_list[w] ^= v (since the neighbor v is being disconnected, so XOR out its contribution.)

    # Now check if w's new degree is now 1.

    if degrees[w] ==1:

Processing the edge (v and w). Because v had degree 1. So after processing, v's degree is set to zero. w's degree reduces by one, and s is updated.

+<#sep#>user: What's a good mojito recipe? I don't like my mojitos too sweat so keep the sugar to a minimum.

assistant: Here's a simple mojito recipe that you can adjust to your liking:

Ingredients:
- 2 oz white rum
- 1 oz lime juice
- 1 tsp granulated sugar (or less, to taste)
- 6-8 fresh mint leaves
- Club soda
- Ice

Directions:
1. In a cocktail shaker, muddle the mint leaves with the sugar and lime juice until the leaves are bruised and the sugar has dissolved.
2. Add the rum and some ice to the shaker and shake well.
3. Fill a glass with ice and strain the contents of the shaker into the glass.
4. Top the glass with club soda and give it a stir.
5. Garnish with a sprig of mint and a lime wedge, if desired.

Note: If you prefer your mojitos less sweet, you can reduce the amount of sugar or skip it altogether. You can also adjust the amount of rum and lime juice to your liking. Enjoy!

user: Can you write me a complete LSTM neural network from scratch while using the PyTorch module?

assistant: Sure! Here's an example implementation of a simple LSTM neural network using PyTorch:

```py
import torch
import torch.nn as nn

class LSTM(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self, input_size, hidden_size, num_layers, output_size):
        super().__init__()
        self.hidden_size = hidden_size
        self.num_layers = num_layers
        self.lstm = nn.LSTM(input_size, hidden_size, num_layers, batch_first=True)
        self.fc = nn.Linear(hidden_size, output_size)
        
    def forward(self, x):
        h0 = torch.zeros(self.num_layers, x.size(0), self.hidden_size).to(x.device)
        c0 = torch.zeros(self.num_layers, x.size(0), self.hidden_size).to(x.device)
        
        out, _ = self.lstm(x, (h0, c0))
        out = out[:, -1, :]
        out = self.fc(out)
        
        return out
```
This implementation defines an LSTM class that inherits from nn.Module. The constructor takes four arguments: input_size, hidden_size, num_layers, and output_size. These parameters determine the size of the input and output layers, the number of LSTM layers, and the number of output neurons, respectively.

The __init__ method sets up the LSTM layer using nn.LSTM, and sets up a fully connected layer using nn.Linear. The forward method takes the input tensor x and passes it through the LSTM layer, then through the fully connected layer to produce the output tensor.

To use this LSTM, you can instantiate an object of the LSTM class and pass input data to it as follows:

```py
input_size = 10
hidden_size = 20
num_layers = 2
output_size = 1

model = LSTM(input_size, hidden_size, num_layers, output_size)
x = torch.randn(1, 3, 10) # input tensor of shape (batch_size, sequence_length, input_size)
output = model(x)
```
This will create an LSTM with 2 layers, 10 input neurons, 20 hidden neurons, and 1 output neuron. The input tensor x has shape (1, 3, 10), which means that there is one sample with three timesteps and 10 input values at each timestep. The output tensor output has shape (1, 1), which means that there is one sample with one output value.

Note that this implementation is a simple example and may need to be modified to suit your specific use case.<#sep#>-

Squaring both sides:

(x -19)^2 + (y -12)^2 = 90.

So, now we have two equations:

1. 4x -3y =25,

2. (x -19)^2 + (y -12)^2 =90.

We can solve these equations simultaneously to find the coordinates (x,y) of point M, then compute AM = sqrt(x² + y²).

5x² -166x +1232 =0.

160²=25600,

6²=36,

</think>To solve the problem of finding the length of \(AM\) in the parallelogram \(ABCD\), we will follow a systematic approach.

### Step 1: Determine the Coordinates of the Vertices
Given:
- \(AB = 10\) cm
- \(BC = 15\) cm
- \(\cos \angle DAB = \frac{3}{5}\)

We place \(A\) at the origin \((0,0)\) and \(B\) at \((10,0)\).

Using the cosine rule:
- The x-coordinate of \(D\) is \(AD \cdot \cos \angle DAB = 15 \cdot \frac{3}{5} = 9\).
- The y-coordinate of \(D\) is \(AD \cdot \sin \angle DAB = 15 \cdot \frac{4}{5} = 12\).

Thus, the coordinates of \(D\) are \((9, 12)\).

To find the coordinates of \(C\), we use the fact that \(C = B + \overrightarrow{AD}\):
- \(\overrightarrow{AD} = (9, 12)\)
- \(C = (10, 0) + (9, 12) = (19, 12)\)

So, the coordinates of the vertices are:
- \(A(0,0)\)
- \(B(10,0)\)
- \(D(9,12)\)
- \(C(19,12)\)

### Step 2: Equation of Line \(AD\)
The equation of line \(AD\) is:
\[ y = \frac{4}{3}x \]
or in standard form:
\[ 4x - 3y = 0 \]

Now, considering the time complexity.

Each edge is processed once, right?

Each time an edge is added, it corresponds to exactly one node being dequeued. Each node can be added to the queue multiple times only if its degree reduces from higher to 1 again. 

The time is O(n) for the processing since each node can be enqueued at most degree - 0 times? Well, each node can be enqueued up to its degree time? Hmm.

Wait the maximum number of times a node is enqueued would be one less than its degree.

Wait each time, when considering a node, after processing, it’s degree is reduced by 1. But a leaf is added once on the queue once. When a node's degree becomes 1 again, it is added again. 

Thus for a node in a chain (binary tree), its degree could be 2 (non-leaf) then become 1 (when one neighbor is processed) so it gets added to queue. So each edge processing reduces a degree.

The total steps would be O(m), since each edge is processed once (original degree plus the steps). Since m is the number of edges, which is up to n-1, so this is manageable for n up to 2^16.

new file mode 100644
index 0000000..ca407cd

+++ b/pykusto/type_utils.py
@@ -0,0 +1,65 @@
+from datetime import datetime, timedelta
+from numbers import Number
+from typing import Union, Mapping, Type, Dict, Callable, Tuple, List
+
+KustoTypes = Union[str, Number, bool, datetime, Mapping, List, Tuple, timedelta]
+# TODO: Unhandled data types: guid, decimal
+
+
+class TypeRegistrar:
+    """
+    A factory for annotations that are used to create a mapping between Kusto types and python types and functions.
+    Each annotation must be called with a Kusto type as a parameter. The `for_obj` and `for_type` methods
+    can then be used to retrieve the python type or function corresponding to a given Kusto type.
+    """
+    registry: Dict[Type[KustoTypes], Callable]
+
+    def __init__(self, name: str) -> None:
+        """
+        :param name: Name is used for better logging and clearer errors
+        """
+        self.name = name
+        self.registry = {}
+
+    def __repr__(self) -> str:
+        return self.name
+
+    def __call__(self, *types: Type[KustoTypes]) -> Callable:
+        def inner(wrapped):
+            for t in types:
+                previous = self.registry.setdefault(t, wrapped)
+                if previous is not wrapped:
+                    raise TypeError("{}: type already registered: {}".format(self, t.__name__))
+            return wrapped
+
+        return inner
+
+    def for_obj(self, obj: KustoTypes) -> Callable:
+        """
+        Given an object of Kusto type, retrieve the python type or function associated with the object's type, and call
+        it with the given object as a parameter
+
+        :param obj: An object of Kusto type
+        :return: Associated python object
+        """
+        for registered_type, registered_callable in self.registry.items():
+            if isinstance(obj, registered_type):
+                return registered_callable(obj)
+        raise ValueError("{}: no registered callable for object {} of type {}".format(self, obj, type(obj).__name__))
+
+    def for_type(self, t: Type[KustoTypes]) -> Callable:
+        """
+        Given a Kusto type, retrieve the associated python type or function
+
+        :param t: A Kusto type
+        :return: Associated python object
+        """
+        for registered_type, registered_callable in self.registry.items():
+            if issubclass(t, registered_type):
+                return registered_callable
+        raise ValueError("{}: no registered callable for type {}".format(self, t.__name__))
+
+
+kql_converter = TypeRegistrar("KQL Converter")
+plain_expression = TypeRegistrar("Plain expression")
+aggregation_expression = TypeRegistrar("Aggregation expression")

import sys
from functools import lru_cache

def main():
    sys.setrecursionlimit(1 << 25)
    N = int(sys.stdin.readline())
    size = 2 * N
    A = []
    for _ in range(size):
        A.append(sys.stdin.readline().strip())
    
    # Precompute allowed pairs
    allowed = [[] for _ in range(size)]
    for i in range(size):
        for j in range(i+1, size):
            if A[i][j] == '1':
                allowed[i].append(j)
    
    # Precompute cross pairs
    cross_table = dict()
    for i in range(size):
        for j in allowed[i]:
            for k in range(size):
                for l in allowed[k]:
                    if k >= i or (k == i and l <= j):
                        continue
                    a, b = sorted([i, j])
                    c, d = sorted([k, l])
                    cross = (c < a < d < b) or (a < c < b < d)
                    cross_table[(i, j, k, l)] = cross
                    cross_table[(k, l, i, j)] = cross
    
    @lru_cache(maxsize=None)
    def dp(mask, components, edges):
        if bin(mask).count('1') == size:
            return 1 if (edges == N-1 and components == 1) else 0
        
        res = 0
        # Find the first unmatched point
        first = 0
        while (mask >> first) & 1:
            first += 1
        
        for second in allowed[first]:
            if (mask >> second) & 1:
                continue
            new_mask = mask | (1 << first) | (1 << second)
            new_components = components + 1
            new_edges = edges
            cross_cnt = 0
            # Check crossings with existing pairs
            existing_pairs = []
            for i in range(size):
                if not (mask & (1 << i)):
                    continue
                for j in allowed[i]:
                    if i < j and (mask & (1 << j)):
                        existing_pairs.append((i, j))
            
            for (i, j) in existing_pairs:
                if cross_table.get((i, j, first, second), False):
                    cross_cnt += 1
            
            if cross_cnt == 0:
                new_components = components + 1
                new_edges = edges
            else:
                new_components = components - cross_cnt + 1
                new_edges = edges + cross_cnt
            
            if new_edges > N-1:
                continue
            if new_components < 1:
                continue
            
            res += dp(new_mask, new_components, new_edges)
        
        return res
    
    print(dp(0, 0, 0))

if __name__ == '__main__':
    main()<#sep#>Please answer the given financial question based on the context.

### Step 2: Equation of Line \(AD\)
The equation of line \(AD\) is:
\[ y = \frac{4}{3}x \]
or in standard form:
\[ 4x - 3y = 0 \]

### Step 3: Distance from Point \(M\) to Line \(AD\)
Given that the distance from \(M(x, y)\) to line \(AD\) is 5 cm:
\[ \frac{|4x - 3y|}{5} = 5 \]
\[ |4x - 3y| = 25 \]

Since \(M\) is inside the parallelogram, the valid equation is:
\[ 4x - 3y = 25 \]

### Step 4: Distance from \(M\) to \(C\)
Given that \(MC = 3\sqrt{10}\):
\[ \sqrt{(x - 19)^2 + (y - 12)^2} = 3\sqrt{10} \]
\[ (x - 19)^2 + (y - 12)^2 = 90 \]

### Step 5: Solve the System of Equations
From \(4x - 3y = 25\), we solve for \(y\):
\[ y = \frac{4x - 25}{3} \]

Substitute \(y\) into the distance equation:
\[ (x - 19)^2 + \left(\frac{4x - 61}{3}\right)^2 = 90 \]
\[ (x - 19)^2 + \frac{(4x - 61)^2}{9} = 90 \]

Multiply through by 9 to clear the fraction:
\[ 9(x - 19)^2 + (4x - 61)^2 = 810 \]
\[ 9(x^2 - 38x + 361) + (16x^2 - 488x + 3721) = 810 \]
\[ 9x^2 - 342x + 3249 + 16x^2 - 488x + 3721 = 810 \]
\[ 25x^2 - 830x + 6970 = 810 \]
\[ 25x^2 - 830x + 6160 = 0 \]

Divide by 5:
\[ 5x^2 - 166x + 1232 = 0 \]

Solve the quadratic equation using the quadratic formula:
\[ x = \frac{166 \pm \sqrt{166^2 - 4 \cdot 5 \cdot 1232}}{2 \cdot 5} \]
\[ x = \frac{166 \pm \sqrt{27556 - 24640}}{10} \]
\[ x = \frac{166 \pm \sqrt{2916}}{10} \]
\[ x = \frac{166 \pm 54}{10} \]
\[ x = \frac{220}{10} = 22 \quad \text{(not valid since it's outside the parallelogram)} \]
\[ x = \frac{112}{10} = 11.2 \]

Substitute \(x = 11.2\) back into \(y = \frac{4x - 25}{3}\):
\[ y = \frac{4(11.2) - 25}{3} = \frac{44.8 - 25}{3} = \frac{19.8}{3} = 6.6 \]

Thus, the coordinates of \(M\) are \(\left(\frac{56}{5}, \frac{33}{5}\right)\).

### Step 6: Calculate \(AM\)
\[ AM = \sqrt{\left(\frac{56}{5}\right)^2 + \left(\frac{33}{5}\right)^2} \]
\[ AM = \sqrt{\frac{3136}{25} + \frac{1089}{25}} \]
\[ AM = \sqrt{\frac{4225}{25}} \]
\[ AM = \sqrt{169} \]
\[ AM = 13 \]

Thus, the length of \(AM\) is \(\boxed{13}\).<#sep#>36, so m is up to 65535.

import sys

from collections import deque

def main():

    n = int(sys.stdin.readline())

    degrees = [0] * n

    s_list = [0] *n

    for i in range(n):

        d, s = map(int, sys.stdin.readline().split())

        degrees[i] = d

        s_list[i] = s

    q = deque()

    for i in range(n):

        if degrees[i] ==1:

            q.append(i)

    edges = []

    while q:

        v = q.popleft()

        # check if degree is still 1, in case?

        if degrees[v] !=1:

            continue

        w = s_list[v]

        edges.append( (v, w) )

        # subtract this edge from both ends? No, but the processing is only necessary for w.

        degrees[w] -=1

        s_list[w] ^= v

        if degrees[w] ==1:

            q.append(w)

    # Now, edges are all found? 

    # Output them:

    print(len(edges))

    for a,b in edges:

        print(a, b)

assistant: Te dejo un listado de consejos útiles para ayudar a una persona con discapacidad visual,:
* Si ves por la calle a una persona con bastón blanco o verde y crees que puede necesitar ayuda (porque está parada en una esquina, en una parada de autobús, en el andén de un tren o metro), acércate y pregúntale si necesita ayuda. No te enfades si rechaza tu petición.
* Evita tocarle antes de estar seguro de que te ha escuchado, de lo contrario podría asustarse.
* Para guiarle, no le cojas del brazo ni obstaculices el movimiento del bastón, simplemente da un paso adelante y ofrécele tu hombro o codo.
* Indíquele con la voz si hay obstáculos en el camino, como pozos, aceras rotas, ramas de árboles, etc.
* Si hay una escalera, dile si sube o baja y el número aproximado de escalones.
* Recuerda que el bastón solo detecta obstáculos de cintura para abajo. Si ves que una persona con bastón blanco o verde está a punto de cruzar un obstáculo que el bastón no detecta, comunícaselo
* Para dirigirse a una persona ciega o deficiente, visual, no es necesario levantar la voz.
* Para ayudarle a subir al autobús, anticipa la acción con tu voz, guía tu mano hacia el pasamanos.
* Para ayudarle a sentarse, anticipando la acción con tu voz, guía su mano hacia el respaldo del asiento.
* Avísale cuando llegues y te vayas, para que no se quede hablando solo.
* Si manejas dinero, es posible que tengas que ayudarle a conocer su valor.
* Las plazas y los espacios abiertos suelen desorientar a las personas ciegas, ya que no tienen puntos de referencia como una pared.
* Considera que hay ciertas palabras que, para tener sentido, requieren información visual. Por ejemplo, "aquí", "allí", "allá". En la medida de lo posible, evita utilizarlas. En su lugar, utiliza expresiones como "a la derecha", "a la izquierda", "adelante", "atrás", etc.
* Si vas caminando por la calle y ves venir a una persona ciega, no permanezcas en silencio, ya que esto le hará pensar que no hay nadie en la calzada. En la medida de lo posible, despeja el espacio por donde camina la persona ciega. Si no es posible, pídele que se detenga.
* Si compartes un espacio físico con una persona con discapacidad visual (oficina, casa, etc.), evita dejar obstáculos como cajones abiertos, sillas o cualquier otro objeto que obstruya el paso. Si te ves obligado a obstruir el paso, házselo saber, ya que suelen caminar sin bastón por lugares que les son familiares.
* Cuando te comuniques por correo electrónico o mensaje de texto, evita utilizar abreviaturas confusas ("x" en lugar de "por"). Ten en cuenta que la persona accederá a la información a través del canal auditivo.<#sep#>-

First, I remember that inequalities like this often use techniques like the AM-GM inequality, where you can set up expressions to find a minimum or maximum. Since we're dealing with exponents 3 and 5, and the product \( xy \), maybe AM-GM can help here. But I need to make sure that the inequality holds for all positive \( x \) and \( y \), so we have to find the smallest \( k \) such that no matter what \( x \) and \( y \) we pick, the left side is always at least the right side.

Alternatively, another way to approach this could be to consider the function \( f(x, y) = x^3 + y^5 - xy \), and then find the global minimum of this function over all positive \( x \) and \( y \). Then, the minimal \( k \) would be the negative of that minimum (since \( k \) has to be such that \( x^3 + y^5 + k \ge xy \), which is equivalent to \( k \ge xy - x^3 - y^5 \), so the minimal \( k \) is the supremum of \( xy - x^3 - y^5 \), but actually, since \( k \) has to be greater than or equal to \( xy - x^3 - y^5 \) for all \( x, y \), then \( k \) needs to be at least the maximum value that \( xy - x^3 - y^5 \) can take. Wait, the inequality is \( x^3 + y^5 + k \geq xy \). To make this hold for all \( x, y >0 \), then \( k \geq xy - x^3 - y^5 \) must hold for all \( x, y >0 \). Therefore, the minimal such \( k \) is the supremum (least upper bound) of \( xy - x^3 - y^5 \) over all positive \( x, y \). Therefore, we need to find \( \sup_{x,y>0} [xy - x^3 - y^5] \), and then set \( k \) equal to that. So, the problem reduces to finding the maximum value of the function \( f(x,y) = xy - x^3 - y^5 \).

Therefore, my task is to find the global maximum of \( f(x, y) \), and that will be the minimal \( k \).

To find the maximum of this function, I can use calculus. So first, let's set up partial derivatives.

Compute the partial derivatives of \( f \):

\( f_x = \frac{\partial f}{\partial x} = y - 3x^2 \)

\( f_y = \frac{\partial f}{\partial y} = x - 5y^4 \)

Set these partial derivatives equal to zero to find critical points.

So, critical points satisfy:

1. \( y - 3x^2 = 0 \implies y = 3x^2 \)

2. \( x - 5y^4 = 0 \implies x = 5y^4 \)

\( x = 5 \times 81x^8 = 405x^8 \)

\( 405x^8 - x = 0 \)

\( x(405x^7 - 1) = 0 \)

\( 405x^7 - 1 = 0 \implies x^7 = 1/405 \implies x = (1/405)^{1/7} \)

```python
import sys
from collections import deque

def main():
    n = int(sys.stdin.readline())
    degrees = [0] * n
    s_list = [0] * n
    for i in range(n):
        d, s = map(int, sys.stdin.readline().split())
        degrees[i] = d
        s_list[i] = s

    q = deque()
    for i in range(n):
        if degrees[i] == 1:
            q.append(i)

    edges = []

    while q:
        v = q.popleft()
        if degrees[v] != 1:
            continue
        w = s_list[v]
        edges.append((v, w))
        # Update degrees and s for w
        degrees[v] = 0  # This node is now done
        degrees[w] -= 1
        s_list[w] ^= v
        if degrees[w] == 1:
            q.append(w)

    print(len(edges))
    for a, b in edges:
        print(a, b)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))
dp = [[0] * n for _ in range(n)]

for l in range(1, n + 1):
    for i in range(n - l + 1):
        j = i + l - 1
        if i == j:
            dp[i][j] = a[i]
        else:
            left = a[i] - dp[i + 1][j]
            right = a[j] - dp[i][j - 1]
            dp[i][j] = max(left, right)

Or in other words: given n output the (n * 2)-th term of OEIS A017936: smallest number whose square has n digits.
Essentially we removed powers of 10 from mentioned OEIS serie.
Notes
As noted by  Neil, there is a correlation with the digits of √(1/10), or even 10, 1 or any power of 10.
He also explained: the length of integers increases at each power of 10. The length of squares of integers increases at (the ceiling of) each power of √10. Half of these powers are also powers of 10, while the other half are powers of 10 multiplied by √(1/10).
Additionally a proof was given by jjagmath.
If n is the number where the square change from having k digits to \$k+1\$ digits then \$(n-1)^2<10^k\le n^2\$.
So \$n-1 < 10^{k/2}\le n\$.
If k is even we have \$n = 10^{k/2}\$.
If \$k = 2m+1\$ then \$n-1 < 10^{m+1}\sqrt{1/10}< n\$ which explains why the first digits of n coincide with  \$\sqrt{1/10}\$.
More notes

There is a difference between the last digit and the corresponding digit in \$\sqrt{1/10}\$, e.g. for the 5th term:

5th term     31623 
√(1/10)    0.31622776601..
                 ↑


Formal formula from OEIS A017936: a(n) = ceiling(10^((n-1)/2)) which includes powers of 10 terms.

The next term of x is between \$(x-1) * 10\$ and \$x * 10\$


Rules
This is code-golf, all usual golfing rules apply.
This is also sequence so you can output:

n-th element
first n terms
infinite sequence

Floating point issues are allowed.
However answers that doesn't fail due to floating point issues are encouraged, you can post both solutions so that we'll see the difference, in that case please post best score as main solution and the other as alternative indicating both scores.
I could fork the challenge in two separate questions but..   I think duplicating is annoying and doesn't make much sense.
At the same time, considering what @Bubbler stated in the FP issues consensus: "Allowing or disallowing such functions (and intermediate floating point values) has a huge consequence, to the point that I could say it defines the actual challenge", I think it may be interesting to have a kind of challenge inside the challenge between FP issues in the same language.

assistant: APL (A Programming Language) is a high-level, concise, and expressive programming language developed by Kenneth E. Iverson in the 1960s. It is particularly well-suited for mathematical and scientific applications. Some reasons why someone might choose to code in APL are:

Array-oriented programming: APL is designed for working with arrays and matrices, making it an attractive choice for those working with large data sets or mathematical problems that require extensive manipulation of arrays.

Conciseness and expressiveness: APL has a unique and compact syntax that allows for the creation of powerful one-liners and concise code. This can lead to faster prototyping, easier debugging, and more maintainable code.

Interactive environment: APL provides an interactive environment that enables developers to quickly test and refine their code, making it an appealing choice for rapid development and experimentation.

Domain-specific applications: APL has been used extensively in finance, engineering, and other scientific fields where data analysis and mathematical modeling are crucial. For professionals in these domains, APL can be a valuable tool for solving complex problems.

Learning and teaching: APL's mathematical notation and concise syntax can make it an interesting language for teaching programming concepts and mathematical problem-solving.

Legacy systems: Some organizations still maintain legacy systems developed in APL, which require maintenance and updates from developers who are familiar with the language.

However, it is essential to note that APL's unique and concise syntax can also be a barrier to entry for new programmers or those who are used to more conventional languages like Python, Java, or C++. Additionally, APL may not be the best choice for large-scale software development, as it lacks the extensive libraries and community support offered by more popular languages.

def precompute_primes_and_sums():
    primes = [2]
    current = 3
    while len(primes) < 10000:
        is_prime = True
        sqrt_current = current ** 0.5
        for p in primes:
            if p > sqrt_current:
                break
            if current % p == 0:
                is_prime = False
                break
        if is_prime:
            primes.append(current)
        current += 2
    
    sum_list = [0]
    current_sum = 0
    for prime in primes:
        current_sum += prime
        sum_list.append(current_sum)
    return sum_list

sum_list = precompute_primes_and_sums()

while True:
    try:
        n = int(input())
        if n == 0:
            break
        print(sum_list[n])
    except EOFError:

But given that this is a code golf challenge where minimal code is preferred, perhaps the floating point approach might be easier, but would fail for large n. But the user says we can allow such solutions as long as we note them.

So for a code golf answer, perhaps using the formula ceil( (10^(n)) / (sqrt(10)) )) using floating points.

But let's think of possible implementation in various languages.

First, let's see what the required output is: for n, the output must be the nth term of the problem's sequence, which is the (2n)th term of A017936 via the formula ceil(10^{2n-1 over 2}), which is the same as ceil(sqrt(10^{2n-1})).

a(2n) = ceil( 10^{ (2n -1)/2 } ), which is exactly the same as ceil( sqrt(10^{2n -1}) )

Because sqrt(10^(2n-1)) is 10^((2n-1)/2).

So another way to compute that is sqrt(10) * 10^{n-1}

Wait because 10^( (2n -1)/2 ) = 10^{n- 0.5} = (10^{n}) / sqrt(10)

Wait yes:

Thus, the value inside the ceiling function is (10^{n}) / (10^{1/2}).

Wait, sqrt(10) is 10^{1/2}, so yes. Therefore, 10^{n-0.5} = (10^n)/(10^0.5). So the value is (10^n)/sqrt(10).

Therefore, the minimal number is the ceiling of that value. So for n=1: 10 / 3.16227… →3.162 →ceil 4. Correct.

Thus, in code, if I can compute 10^n divided by sqrt(10) and take the ceiling, it would work.

But in programming languages, especially with floating-point precision limits, this could be a problem for large n. Because when n is large, 10^n becomes a huge number. For example, even n=20 would be 10^20, divided by sqrt(10) gives 3.162...e19, but in IEEE double-precision, 1e20 is 1e20 stored as a double, which can represent it but after division by sqrt(10) (approx 3.16227766e0), so it could lose precision in the least significant digits. But perhaps for the purposes here, up to n where 10^n is still within the IEEE double's mantissa precision.

The double can accurately represent integers up to 2^53 exactly. So for n where 10^n is less than 2^53 (~9e15), the value can be stored exactly. For n=15, 1e15 is 1e15, which is within 2^53. However, for n >=16, 10^16 is 1e16 which is beyond 2^53 (~9e15), so the exact value can't be stored, leading to errors.

But the problem allows floating point issues so some leeway is okay. Alternatively, there might be a better way to compute this accurately in code.

Alternatively, we can represent sqrt(10) as a string and perform arithmetic with arbitrary precision.

Alternatively, since the goal is the ceiling of (10^{n}) / sqrt(10), which is the same as the minimal n where x^2 has 2n digits. The problem says that the exact formula is a(m) = ceil(10^{(2n-1)/2}).

Wait, but perhaps an alternative approach is better. For example, the desired x must satisfy that x² >= 10^{2n-1}, since the smallest number's square must have 2n digits (so the minimum starting from 10^{2n-1} up to 10^{2n}-1). Thus, x must be the smallest integer where x^2 >= 10^{2n -1}, so x = ceil( sqrt( 10^{2n-1} )).

Alternatively, we can rephrase that as: x is the smallest integer >= sqrt( 1e(2n-1) )

Which is again the same formula.

assistant: The best time to start fertilizing seedlings depends on the type of fertilizer you are using. Follow the instructions on your fertilizer package and err on the side of caution, using less rather than more.

If you are using a slow-release fertilizer, you can mix it into the potting mix or soil before planting your seeds. This will provide nutrients to the seedlings as they grow.
If you are using a liquid fertilizer, you should wait until the seedlings have developed their first set of true leaves before applying it. At this stage, the seedlings will have used up most of the nutrients stored in the seed and will benefit from additional nutrients.

In general, it's important not to over-fertilize seedlings, as this can cause them to become leggy and weak.

assistant: The relationship between the amount of fertilizer and water given to plants and the frequency of providing water is complex and dependent on several factors, including the type of plant, the type of fertilizer, the growing medium, and environmental conditions.

In general, plants require water to uptake nutrients from the soil and to transport those nutrients to various parts of the plant. Fertilizer provides those essential nutrients to plants that are not present in sufficient quantities in the soil. However, giving too much fertilizer or water can be harmful to plants, as it can cause root burn or root rot.

The frequency of providing water depends on the moisture needs of the plant and the rate at which water evaporates from the growing medium. Some plants require more water than others, and the amount of water required can vary depending on environmental conditions such as temperature, humidity, and wind. The type of soil or growing medium also plays a role in determining how often to water plants, as some soils hold moisture better than others.

When fertilizing plants, it is important to follow the recommended application rate and timing for the specific fertilizer being used. Over-fertilizing can cause excess nutrients to build up in the soil, which can harm the plant's roots and reduce its ability to take up water. Therefore, the frequency of providing water may need to be adjusted when fertilizing to avoid over-saturating the soil and causing nutrient leaching.

Overall, the relationship between fertilizer and water amount and frequency of providing water is complex and requires careful consideration of the specific needs of each plant. It is important to monitor plants regularly and adjust fertilizer and watering practices as needed to ensure healthy growth and development.<#sep#>-

import sys

def main():
    import sys
    from sys import stdin

    input = stdin.read().splitlines()
    N = int(input[0])
    cases = input[1:N+1]

    for case in cases:
        pieces_str = case.split()
        pieces_rotations = []
        for s in pieces_str:
            rotations = []
            # Generate all four rotations for each piece
            rotations.append((s[0], s[1], s[2], s[3]))  # 0 degrees
            rotations.append((s[3], s[0], s[1], s[2]))  # 90 degrees
            rotations.append((s[2], s[3], s[0], s[1]))  # 180 degrees
            rotations.append((s[1], s[2], s[3], s[0]))  # 270 degrees
            pieces_rotations.append(rotations)

        def backtrack(mask, row, col, current_edges, prev_rows_bottom):
            if row == 3:
                return 1
            total = 0
            for piece_idx in range(9):
                if mask & (1 << piece_idx):
                    continue
                for rotation in range(4):
                    top, right, bottom, left = pieces_rotations[piece_idx][rotation]
                    # Check top constraint
                    if row > 0:
                        if top != prev_rows_bottom[row-1][col]:
                            continue
                    # Check left constraint
                    if col > 0:
                        if left != current_edges[col-1][0]:
                            continue
                    new_mask = mask | (1 << piece_idx)
                    new_current_edges = current_edges.copy()
                    new_current_edges.append((right, bottom))
                    if col == 2:
                        new_prev = prev_rows_bottom.copy()
                        new_prev.append([e[1] for e in new_current_edges])
                        total += backtrack(new_mask, row + 1, 0, [], new_prev)
                    else:
                        total += backtrack(new_mask, row, col + 1, new_current_edges, prev_rows_bottom)
            return total

        count = backtrack(0, 0, 0, [], [])
        print(count)

if __name__ == "__main__":

import sys
from itertools import product

def generate_rotations(cube):
    def roll(v): return (v[0], v[2], v[4], v[1], v[3], v[5])
    def turn(v): return (v[2], v[0], v[1], v[3], v[4], v[5])
    def sequence(v):
        for _ in range(2):
            for _ in range(3):
                v = roll(v)
                yield v
                for _ in range(3):
                    v = turn(v)
                    yield v
            v = roll(turn(roll(v)))

    start = (0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5)
    seen = set()
    rotations = []
    for perm in sequence(start):
        if perm not in seen:
            seen.add(perm)
            rotations.append(perm)
    
    rotated = []
    for perm in rotations:
        rotated_cube = [cube[perm[i]] for i in range(6)]
        if rotated_cube not in rotated:
            rotated.append(rotated_cube)
    return rotated

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.read().split()
    ptr = 0
    while True:
        if ptr >= len(input):
            break
        n = int(input[ptr])
        ptr +=1
        if n ==0:
            break
        cubes = []
        for _ in range(n):
            cube = input[ptr:ptr+6]
            ptr +=6
            cubes.append(cube)
        
        face_colors = [{} for _ in range(6)]
        for cube in cubes:
            rotations = generate_rotations(cube)
            per_face = [set() for _ in range(6)]
            for rot in rotations:
                for i in range(6):
                    per_face[i].add(rot[i])
            for i in range(6):
                for c in per_face[i]:
                    face_colors[i][c] = face_colors[i].get(c, 0) + 1
        
        total =0
        for i in range(6):
            if not face_colors[i]:
                total +=n
            else:
                max_count = max(face_colors[i].values())
                total += (n - max_count)
        print(total)

if __name__ == "__main__":

+from abc import ABCMeta, abstractmethod
+from concurrent.futures import Future, ThreadPoolExecutor, wait
+from itertools import chain
+from threading import Lock
+from typing import Union, Dict, Any, Iterable, Callable
+
+# Using a thread pool even though we only need one thread, because that's the only way to make use of "futures".
+# Also, this makes it easy to use more than one thread, if the need ever arises.
+POOL = ThreadPoolExecutor(max_workers=1)
+
+
+class ItemFetcher(metaclass=ABCMeta):
+    """
+    Abstract class that caches a collection of items, fetching them in certain scenarios.
+    """
+    _fetch_by_default: bool
+    __items: Union[None, Dict[str, Any]]
+    __future: Union[None, Future]
+    __item_write_lock: Lock
+    __item_fetch_lock: Lock
+
+    def __init__(self, items: Union[None, Dict[str, Any]], fetch_by_default: bool) -> None:
+        """
+        :param items: Initial items. If not None, items will not be fetched until the "refresh" method is explicitly called.
+        :param fetch_by_default: When true, items will be fetched in the constructor, but only if they were not supplied as a parameter. Subclasses are encouraged to pass
+                                    on the value of "fetch_by_default" to child ItemFetchers.
+        """
+        self._fetch_by_default = fetch_by_default
+        self.__items = items
+        self.__future = None
+        self.__item_write_lock = Lock()
+        self.__item_fetch_lock = Lock()
+        if items is None and fetch_by_default:
+            self.refresh()
+
+    def get_item_names(self):
+        return tuple(self.__items.keys())
+
+    @abstractmethod
+    def _new_item(self, name: str) -> Any:
+        raise NotImplementedError()
+
+    def __getattr__(self, name: str) -> Any:
+        """
+        Convenience function for obtaining an item using dot notation.
+        Often dot notation is used for other purposes, and sometimes that happens implicitly. For example Jupyter notebooks automatically run dot-notation code in the background
+        on objects. For this reason, to avoid undesired erroneous queries sent to Kusto, an item is returned only if one already exists, and a new item is not generated otherwise
+        (in contrast to bracket notation).
+
+        :param name: Name of item to return
+        :return: The item with the given name
+        :raises AttributeError: If there is no such item
+        """
+        return self._get_item(name, lambda: _raise(AttributeError(f"{self} has no attribute '{name}'")))
+
+    def __getitem__(self, name: str) -> Any:

def split_tokens(s):
    tokens = []
    current = []
    inside = False
    for c in s:
        if not inside:
            if c == '"':
                if current:
                    tokens.append(''.join(current))
                    current = []
                current.append(c)
                inside = True
            else:
                current.append(c)
        else:
            current.append(c)
            if c == '"':
                tokens.append(''.join(current))
                current = []
                inside = False
    if not inside and current:
        tokens.append(''.join(current))
    return tokens

def judge(s1, s2):
    tokens1 = split_tokens(s1)
    tokens2 = split_tokens(s2)
    if len(tokens1) != len(tokens2):
        return "DIFFERENT"
    for t1, t2 in zip(tokens1, tokens2):
        is_str1 = t1.startswith('"') and t1.endswith('"')
        is_str2 = t2.startswith('"') and t2.endswith('"')
        if is_str1 != is_str2:
            return "DIFFERENT"
    for t1, t2 in zip(tokens1, tokens2):
        if not t1.startswith('"'):
            if t1 != t2:
                return "DIFFERENT"
    diff = 0
    for t1, t2 in zip(tokens1, tokens2):
        if t1.startswith('"'):
            if t1 != t2:
                diff += 1
    if diff == 0:
        return "IDENTICAL"
    elif diff == 1:
        return "CLOSE"
    else:
        return "DIFFERENT"

import sys

for line in sys.stdin:
    s1 = line.strip()
    if s1 == '.':
        break
    s2 = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
    print(judge(s1, s2))<#sep#>Please answer the given financial question based on the context.

Question: what was the operating income in 2014
Answer:

Let's start by figuring out what operating income really means. Basically, it's the money a company makes from its regular business operations, but after you subtract the costs of doing business. So, you take what you earn from operations and then take away all the operating expenses. Easy enough, right?

Now, let's look at the year 2014. I see the numbers for that year show $13 in operating revenues, which is essentially what the company earned from its regular activities. And then I see they spent a bit more, like $19 on operating expenses. That's quite a bit more than they earned, unfortunately.

So, to calculate the operating income for 2014, we subtract those expenses from the revenues. If I do the math, $13 minus $19 gives us -$6. Hmm, that means they actually spent more than they earned from their core business activities. Not the best scenario for that year!

Okay, so what's our conclusion? It seems like this company had an unfortunate operating loss of $6 in 2014.

And to make sure we're not missing anything, let's quickly double-check. I see the calculation (-$6 = $13 - $19) checks out perfectly with the numbers provided. So yes, the operating income—or lack thereof, in this case—was definitely -$6 for 2014.<#sep#> from substitution:

a= u^3, b= v^5.

Then equations become:

1) v / u^{2} =3

2) u /v^{4}=5

From first equation: v =3u^2

Substitute into second equation:

u/( (3u^2)^4 )=5 → u/(81u^8)=5 → 1/(81u^7)=5 → 81u^7 =1/5 → u^7=1/(405) → u= (1/405)^{1/7}

Thus, u= (1/405)^{1/7}, so a= u^3= (1/405)^{3/7}

Then v=3u²=3*( (1/405)^{2/7} )

Therefore, b= v^5= [3*(1/405)^{2/7}]^5= 3^5*(1/405)^{10/7}=243*(1/405)^{10/7}

Thus, compute f(a,b)=a^{1/3}b^{1/5} -a -b.

Now,

a^{1/3}=u= (1/405)^{1/7}

b^{1/5}=v=3u²=3*(1/405)^{2/7}

So a^{1/3}b^{1/5}= u*v= (1/405)^{1/7} *3*(1/405)^{2/7}=3*(1/405)^{3/7}

Then subtract a and b:

-a = - (1/405)^{3/7}

-b= -243*(1/405)^{10/7}

Thus,

f(a,b)=3*(1/405)^{3/7} - (1/405)^{3/7} -243*(1/405)^{10/7}

Simplify the first two terms:

(3-1)*(1/405)^{3/7}=2*(1/405)^{3/7}

Third term: -243*(1/405)^{10/7}= -243*(1/405)^{3/7}*(1/405)^{7/7}= -243*(1/405)^{3/7}*(1/405)

Thus,

f(a,b)=2*(1/405)^{3/7} -243/(405)*(1/405)^{3/7}

Compute 243/405 = 0.6 = 3/5 as before,

Thus,

f(a,b)= (2 - 3/5)*(1/405)^{3/7}= (7/5)*(1/405)^{3/7}=7/(5*405^{3/7})

Same result as before. So that's correct.

Thus, the minimal k is exactly \( \dfrac{7}{5 \cdot 405^{3/7}} \).

Since I can't simplify this further without approximation, I think that is the answer. Therefore, I should box this expression.

**Final Answer**
\boxed{\dfrac{7}{5 \cdot 405^{3/7}}}
</think>To find the minimum real number \( k \) such that for all positive real numbers \( x \) and \( y \), the inequality \( x^3 + y^5 + k \geq xy \) holds, we need to determine the maximum value of the function \( f(x, y) = xy - x^3 - y^5 \). The minimum \( k \) will be this maximum value.

First, we compute the partial derivatives of \( f \):
\[
f_x = y - 3x^2 \quad \text{and} \quad f_y = x - 5y^4
\]

Setting these partial derivatives to zero to find critical points:
1. \( y - 3x^2 = 0 \implies y = 3x^2 \)
2. \( x - 5y^4 = 0 \implies x = 5y^4 \)

Substituting \( y = 3x^2 \) into \( x = 5y^4 \):
\[
x = 5(3x^2)^4 = 5 \cdot 81x^8 = 405x^8
\]
\[
x - 405x^8 = 0 \implies x(1 - 405x^7) = 0
\]
Since \( x > 0 \), we have:
\[
405x^7 = 1 \implies x^7 = \frac{1}{405} \implies x = \left( \frac{1}{405} \right)^{1/7}
\]

Substituting \( x = \left( \frac{1}{405} \right)^{1/7} \) back into \( y = 3x^2 \):
\[
y = 3 \left( \left( \frac{1}{405} \right)^{1/7} \right)^2 = 3 \left( \frac{1}{405} \right)^{2/7}
\]

Evaluating \( f(x, y) \) at this critical point:
\[
f(x, y) = xy - x^3 - y^5
\]
\[
xy = x \cdot 3x^2 = 3x^3
\]
\[
x^3 = \left( \left( \frac{1}{405} \right)^{1/7} \right)^3 = \left( \frac{1}{405} \right)^{3/7}
\]
\[
y^5 = \left( 3 \left( \frac{1}{405} \right)^{2/7} \right)^5 = 243 \left( \frac{1}{405} \right)^{10/7}
\]
\[
f(x, y) = 3x^3 - x^3 - 243x^{10} = 2x^3 - 243x^{10}
\]
Since \( x^7 = \frac{1}{405} \), we have \( x^{10} = x^7 \cdot x^3 = \frac{1}{405} x^3 \):
\[
f(x, y) = 2x^3 - 243 \left( \frac{1}{405} x^3 \right) = 2x^3 - \frac{243}{405} x^3 = \left( 2 - \frac{3}{5} \right) x^3 = \frac{7}{5} x^3
\]
\[
x^3 = \left( \frac{1}{405} \right)^{3/7}
\]
\[
f(x, y) = \frac{7}{5} \left( \frac{1}{405} \right)^{3/7} = \frac{7}{5 \cdot 405^{3/7}}
\]

Thus, the minimum real number \( k \) is:
\[
\boxed{\dfrac{7}{5 \cdot 405^{3/7}}}
\]<#sep#> target=10.

+        (in contrast to bracket notation).
+
+        :param name: Name of item to return
+        :return: The item with the given name
+        :raises AttributeError: If there is no such item
+        """
+        return self._get_item(name, lambda: _raise(AttributeError(f"{self} has no attribute '{name}'")))
+
+    def __getitem__(self, name: str) -> Any:
+        """
+        Convenience function for obtaining an item using bracket notation.
+        Since bracket notation is only used explicitly, a new item is generated if needed (in contrast to dot notation).
+
+        :param name: Name of item to return
+        :return: The item with the given name
+        """
+        return self._get_item(name, lambda: self.__generate_and_save_new_item(name))
+
+    def __generate_and_save_new_item(self, name):
+        item = self._new_item(name)
+        with self.__item_write_lock:
+            if self.__items is None:
+                self.__items = {}
+            self.__items[name] = item
+        return item
+
+    def _get_item(self, name: str, fallback: Callable) -> Any:
+        if self.__items is not None:
+            resolved_item = self.__items.get(name)
+            if resolved_item is not None:
+                return resolved_item
+        return fallback()
+
+    def __dir__(self) -> Iterable[str]:
+        return sorted(chain(super().__dir__(), tuple() if self.__items is None else self.__items.keys()))
+
+    def refresh(self):
+        """
+        Fetches all items in a separate thread, making them available after the tread finishes executing. The 'wait_for_items' method can be used to wait for that to happen.
+        The specific logic for fetching is defined in concrete subclasses.
+        """
+        self.__future = POOL.submit(self._get_items)
+        self.__future.add_done_callback(self._set_items)
+
+    def wait_for_items(self):
+        """
+        If item fetching is currently in progress, wait until it is done and return, otherwise return immediately.
+        If several fetching threads are in progress, wait for the most recent one.
+        """
+        if self.__future is not None:
+            wait((self.__future,))
+
+    def _set_items(self, future: Future):
+        with self.__item_write_lock:
+            self.__items = future.result()
+
+    @abstractmethod
+    def _internal_get_items(self) -> Dict[str, Any]:
+        raise NotImplementedError()
+
+    def _get_items(self) -> Dict[str, Any]:
+        with self.__item_fetch_lock:
+            return self._internal_get_items()
+
+
+def _raise(e: BaseException):
+    raise e

        sum_original = 0.0
        endpoints = []
        for _ in range(n):
            x1 = float(input[ptr])
            y1 = float(input[ptr+1])
            x2 = float(input[ptr+2])
            y2 = float(input[ptr+3])
            ptr += 4
            sum_original += math.hypot(x1 - x2, y1 - y2)
            endpoints.append((x1, y1))
            endpoints.append((x2, y2))
        
        masks_by_bits = [[] for _ in range(n + 1)]
        for mask in range(1 << n):
            cnt = bin(mask).count('1')
            masks_by_bits[cnt].append(mask)
        
        INF = 1e18
        size_mask = 1 << n
        size_eps = 2 * n
        dp = [[INF] * size_eps for _ in range(size_mask)]
        for i in range(n):
            for j in (0, 1):
                e = 2 * i + j
                mask = 1 << i
                dp[mask][e] = 0.0
        
        for bits in range(1, n):
            for mask in masks_by_bits[bits]:
                for e in range(size_eps):
                    if dp[mask][e] >= INF:
                        continue
                    for k in range(n):
                        if (mask & (1 << k)) != 0:
                            continue
                        for j in (0, 1):
                            ek = 2 * k + j
                            new_mask = mask | (1 << k)
                            new_e = ek ^ 1
                            x1, y1 = endpoints[e]
                            x2, y2 = endpoints[ek]
                            dist = math.hypot(x1 - x2, y1 - y2)
                            if dp[new_mask][new_e] > dp[mask][e] + dist:
                                dp[new_mask][new_e] = dp[mask][e] + dist
        
        full_mask = (1 << n) - 1
        min_conn = min(dp[full_mask][e] for e in range(size_eps))
        total = sum_original + min_conn
        print(f"Case {case}: {total:.5f}")
        case += 1

Okay, let's tackle this problem. So, the question is asking for the largest positive integer \( n \) such that there exist integer solutions for \( x, y_1, y_2, \ldots, y_n \) where all the equations \((x + 1)^2 + y_1^2 = (x + 2)^2 + y_2^2 = \cdots = (x + n)^2 + y_n^2\) are equal. That means all those expressions must result in the same constant value, right? Let me think about how to approach this.

First, maybe I should start by considering that all these expressions are equal to some common value \( k^2 \), since they are sums of squares and likely need to be perfect squares if we're looking for integer solutions. So, we can set up equations:

For each \( i \) from 1 to \( n \), we have:
\[
(x + i)^2 + y_i^2 = k^2
\]
So, each \( (x + i, y_i, k) \) forms a Pythagorean triple. Since \( x \) is the same for all equations, we need all these triples to share the same hypotenuse \( k \), with one leg being \( x + i \), and the other leg \( y_i \).

Hmm, so essentially, we're trying to find a single hypotenuse \( k \) such that there are \( n \) different integer legs \( x + 1, x + 2, \ldots, x + n \), each paired with another integer leg \( y_i \) to form a Pythagorean triple. The challenge is to find the maximum \( n \) for which such an \( x \) and \( k \) exist.

Let me consider the differences between consecutive terms. For example, the difference between \( (x + i + 1)^2 + y_{i+1}^2 \) and \( (x + i)^2 + y_i^2 \) must be zero since they are all equal. So:

\[
(x + i + 1)^2 + y_{i+1}^2 = (x + i)^2 + y_i^2
\]

\[

\[
2x + 2i + 1 + y_{i+1}^2 - y_i^2 = 0
\]

So:

\[
y_{i+1}^2 - y_i^2 = -2x - 2i - 1
\]

Factor the left side as a difference of squares:

\[

\]

\[

assistant: To determine how many 1/2-inch diameter marbles you can fit in a 1-gallon bucket, you'll need to calculate the volume of a single marble and the volume of the bucket, then divide the bucket's volume by the marble's volume. Marbles won't perfectly fill the entire space, so we'll also need to account for the packing efficiency.

Here is a step-by-step guide:

1. **Calculate the volume of a single marble:**
The formula for the volume of a sphere is V = (4/3)πr^3, where V is the volume and r is the radius. Since the diameter of the marble is 1/2 inch, the radius is 1/4 inch.
V = (4/3)π(1/4)^3
V ≈ 0.06545 cubic inches

2. **Convert the volume of the 1-gallon bucket to cubic inches:**
1 gallon = 231 cubic inches

3. **Calculate the packing efficiency:**
The most efficient way to pack spheres is in a hexagonal close-packed or face-centered cubic arrangement, which has a packing efficiency of about 74% (0.74).

4. **Calculate the effective volume of the bucket:**
Effective volume = Total volume × Packing efficiency
Effective volume = 231 cubic inches × 0.74
Effective volume ≈ 170.94 cubic inches

5. **Divide the effective volume of the bucket by the volume of a single marble:**
Number of marbles = Effective volume / Volume of a single marble
Number of marbles ≈ 170.94 / 0.06545
Number of marbles ≈ 2611

So, you can fit approximately 2611 marbles with a 1/2-inch diameter in a 1-gallon bucket, considering the packing efficiency.

user: How much space, in marbles, is in-between the marbles?

assistant: Since the hexagonal close-packed marbles have a packing efficiency of about 74%, approximately 2611 marbles make up 74% of the bucket, and the other 26% is the space in-between the marbles. The space in-between the marbles in marbles can then be calculated as:
2611 * 0.26/0.74 ≈ 917 marbles.

user: Well now I understand that the packing efficiency is what's needed to answer my question. Can you give me the packing efficiency for more shapes like cubes and pyramids?

assistant: Sure! This is highly relevant to the atomic packing factor (APF) in crystallography, which can be simplified as "If we fill a space with identical rigid spheres in highly ordered a structure, what is the fraction of volume that is occupied the spheres". To calculate APF, we usually use a unit cell to represent the space to be filled, which is the smallest portion of the space that can make up it by being repeated over and over in three dimensions. Note that spheres in a unit cell can be shared by multiple adjacent unit cells. For example, a sphere on the face of a unit cell is shared by two adjacent cells having this same face, and the space it occupies in one unit cell should be half of its volume. Here are the APFs, or packing efficiencies of some common structures:

- Simple cubic. It is a cubic unit cell with 8 adjacent spheres on its 8 vertices, and each sphere is shared among 8 cells having this same vertex. So if the radius of the sphere is r, its side length is 2r, leading to an APF of about 0.52.

- Face centered cubic. It is a cubic unit cell with 8 spheres on its 8 vertices, as well as 6 spheres on its 6 faces. You can also think of this as placing a layer of spheres on the cross points of a square grid with a side length of 2r, and placing the spheres of the second layer in the center points of the grid of the first layer, then repeating this process to fill up a space. Itis has an APF of about 0.74.

- Body-centered cubic.  It is a cubic unit cell with 8 spheres on its 8 vertices and one in its center. It has an APF of about 0.68.

- Hexagonal close-packed. The unit cell is a hexagonal prism. There are 12 spheres on its vertices and 2 on the centers of its top and bottom faces. There are another 3 sphere inside it. If we vertically divide it into 6 identical triangular prisms, these 3 spheres are in the center of 3 of the 6 prisms separated by one. You can also think of this as placing a layer of spheres on the cross points of a triangle grid with a side length of 2r, and placing the spheres of the second layer in the center points of the grid of the first layer, then repeating this process to fill up a space. It has an APF of about 0.74.

- Diamond cubic. It is also a cubic unit cell, but the locations of the spheres is hard to describe with words. An easier way is to explain the basic structure as a tetrahedron with 4 spheres on its 4 vertices and one in the center, and this structure spreads by using the 3 bottom vertices of one tetrahedron as the top vertices of another 3. It has an APF of about 0.34.<#sep#>-

def sum_digits_all(n):
    if n < 1:
        return 0
    s = str(n)
    length = len(s)
    res = 0
    for i in range(length):
        d = int(s[i])
        p = length - i - 1
        lower = int(s[:i]) if i > 0 else 0
        current = d
        higher = int(s[i+1:]) if i < length-1 else 0
        if p > 0:
            res += (lower) * 10**p * 9 // 9 * 45
        res += (current - 1) * (current) // 2 * 10**p
        res += current * (higher + 1)
        res += sum(range(current)) * 10**p
    return res

def sum_digits_div_k(n, k):
    from functools import lru_cache
    s = str(n)
    length = len(s)
    
    @lru_cache(maxsize=None)
    def dp(pos, mod, tight, leading_zero):
        if pos == length:
            return 0 if mod == 0 and not leading_zero else 0
        limit = int(s[pos]) if tight else 9
        total = 0
        for d in range(0, limit + 1):
            new_tight = tight and (d == limit)
            new_leading_zero = leading_zero and (d == 0)
            new_mod = (mod * 10 + d) % k if not new_leading_zero else 0
            if new_leading_zero:
                sum_digits = 0
            else:
                sum_digits = d
            if not new_leading_zero and (new_mod % k) == 0:
                cnt = 1
            else:
                cnt = 0
            next_sum = dp(pos + 1, new_mod, new_tight, new_leading_zero)
            total += sum_digits * cnt + next_sum
        return total
    return dp(0, 0, True, True)

def calculate_sum_digits_div_3(n):
    return sum_digits_div_k(n, 3)

def calculate_sum_digits_div_5(n):
    return sum_digits_div_k(n, 5)

def calculate_sum_digits_div_15(n):
    return sum_digits_div_k(n, 15)

def sum_digits_not_div_3_5(n):
    total_all = sum_digits_all(n)
    sum_3 = calculate_sum_digits_div_3(n)
    sum_5 = calculate_sum_digits_div_5(n)
    sum_15 = calculate_sum_digits_div_15(n)
    return total_all - sum_3 - sum_5 + sum_15

def cumulative_length(n):
    count_15 = n // 15
    count_3 = (n // 3) - count_15
    count_5 = (n // 5) - count_15
    fixed = 8 * count_15 + 4 * (count_3 + count_5)
    digits = sum_digits_not_div_3_5(n)
    return fixed + digits

def find_n(s):
    low = 1
    high = 1
    while cumulative_length(high) < s:
        high *= 2
    while low < high:
        mid = (low + high) // 2
        if cumulative_length(mid) < s:
            low = mid + 1
        else:
            high = mid
    return low

def get_fizzbuzz_part(n):
    if n % 15 == 0:
        return "FizzBuzz"
    if n % 3 == 0:
        return "Fizz"
    if n % 5 == 0:
        return "Buzz"
    return str(n)

def solve(s):
    start = s
    s -= 1
    current_pos = 0
    result = []
    n = find_n(s + 1)
    pos_in_n = s - cumulative_length(n-1)
    while len(result) < 20:
        part = get_fizzbuzz_part(n)
        part_len = len(part)
        if pos_in_n >= part_len:
            pos_in_n -= part_len
            n += 1
            continue
        end_pos = min(part_len, pos_in_n + 20 - len(result))
        result.append(part[pos_in_n:end_pos])
        added = end_pos - pos_in_n
        pos_in_n = 0
        n += 1
    return ''.join(result)[:20]

         raise ValueError("{}: no registered callable for type {}".<#sep#>user: Can you give me a generalized form to find all the roots of a polynomial to the n-th degree using the newton method?

assistant: The generalized form of the Newton Method to find the roots of a polynomial to the n-th degree is given by the following equation: 

Xk+1 = Xk - (f(Xk)/f'(Xk))

where Xk is an initial guess and f(Xk) is the value of the polynomial evaluated at Xk, and f'(Xk) is the derivative of the polynomial evaluated at Xk.

user: Can you give me examples?

assistant: Sure, I can give you an example where we can use the Newton Method to approximate the positive square root of two :

0. The polynomial f(x) = x²-2 is the 2nd degree polynomial we are going to use. Simple algebra tells us that its roots are -2^(1/2) and 2^(1/2). In order to use the method, we must compute the derivative of f(x), which is f'(x)=2x
1. The first step is to start with a guess of the value of one root. Usually, this guess is randomized but for the sake of simplicity let's use X0 = 2 where X denotes the root and 0 denotes that this is the starting approximate value of the root. Each successive number X1, X2 and so on will become the better approximate value of the square root of 2
2. The next step is to write the Newton Method formula using the algebraic formes of f(x) and f'(x) : 
Xk+1 = Xk - (f(Xk)/f'(Xk)) then becomes
Xk+1 = Xk - (Xk² -2) / (2 Xk)
This step is useful because we can sometimes simplify the formula using algebra. Each polynomial will have a different simplification but here we can put both termes over the same denominator 2Xk to obtain :
Xk+1 = (2Xk²) / (2Xk) - (Xk² -2) / (2 Xk)
Xk+1 = (2Xk² - Xk² + 2) / (2Xk)
Xk+1 = (Xk² +2) / (2Xk)
3. The next approximate value X1 is given by the formula X1 = X0 - f(X0) / f'(X0). In order to compute this number we must must input the value of X0 in the previous simplified formula in place of Xk. This yields :
X1 = (2² + 2) / (2*2) = 6/4 = 3/2 = 1.5
4. Now we repeat step 3 in a method called iteration where we find each subsequent approximation of X with the help of the previous one. This yields : 
X2 = ( (3/2)² + 2) / (2*(3/2)) = 17/12 = 1.41667
X3 = ( (17/12)² + 2) / (2* (17/12)) = 577/408 = 1.41422

This value X3 is already extremely close to the square root of 2 and is already a good approximation. If we compute the relative distance between the true value X and X3, we find :
(X-X3)/X = ( (577/408) - 2^(1/2) ) / (2^(1/2)) = 1.5E-6
which means that the error margin this approximation creates is about 0.00015 %

Be careful that the number of steps needed will depend on the initial guess. For example in our case where f(x)=x²-2, if we start with the guess x0 = 1000, the number of iterations to beat the previous error margin increases significally. In that case we need to go up to X12.

Be careful as well that this is a simple example and that some edge cases will arise when a polynomial admits multiple multiple roots close to each other, in which case this method may try to approximate two or more values at the same time.<#sep#>-

import sys

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.read().split()
    T = int(input[0])
    idx = 1
    for _ in range(T):
        h = int(input[idx])
        w = int(input[idx+1])
        idx += 2
        if (h + w) % 2 == 0:
            print("1 0")
        else:
            print("1 1")

Double-precision (64-bit) has a 53-bit mantissa, providing about 15-17 decimal digits.

Thus, for 10^(19.5), the value has 20 digits (log10(10^19.5) is 19.5, so it's a 20-digit number). The floating point stores the number with 15-17 digits, meaning the last few digits will be zero.

<#sep#>user: Please suggest three useful applications that can be achieved using autohotkey and irfanview.

assistant: AutoHotkey is a free and open-source scripting language that allows users to automate repetitive tasks and customize their desktops. IrfanView is a lightweight and fast image viewer and editor that supports a wide range of formats. Here are three useful applications that can be achieved by combining AutoHotkey and IrfanView:

1. Batch image editing: With AutoHotkey, you can create scripts to automate repetitive image editing tasks in IrfanView. For example, you can write a script that renames a group of files, converts them to a specific format, and applies a set of filters to each image. This can save a lot of time for photographers, graphic designers, and other professionals who work with large numbers of images.
2. Screen capture and editing: AutoHotkey can be used to create custom keyboard shortcuts for capturing screenshots with IrfanView. Once the screenshot is captured, you can use IrfanView's built-in editing tools to crop, resize, or annotate the image. This can be especially useful for creating tutorials, documentation, or social media content.
3. Image slideshow and presentation: AutoHotkey can be used to create custom scripts for controlling IrfanView's slideshow and presentation modes. For example, you can write a script that automatically plays a slideshow of images in a specific folder, with a specific transition effect and time interval. This can be useful for creating digital signage, presentations, or exhibitions.

These are just a few examples of the many applications that can be achieved with AutoHotkey and IrfanView. The possibilities are endless, and the best way to learn is by experimenting with the tools and exploring the many online resources available for both programs.

user: Can you give me a specific example of how to do one of the above?

assistant: Sure! Here's an example of how to create a simple script in AutoHotkey to automate batch image editing with IrfanView:

Open Notepad or any other plain text editor.
Copy and paste the following code into the editor:

#NoEnv
SendMode Input
SetWorkingDir %A_ScriptDir%

; Define the input and output folders
InputFolder = C:\input\folder
OutputFolder = C:\output\folder

; Set the IrfanView command-line parameters for batch conversion and filtering
IrfanViewPath = C:\Program Files\IrfanView\i_view64.exe
ConversionParams = /convert=%OutputFolder%\*.jpg /jpgq=90 /autoresize=(1280,960)
FilterParams = /advancedbatch /filepattern=*.jpg /autoadjust=0 /jpgq=90 /enhancergb=(40,0,60,20,0,40)

; Convert the images to the output folder and apply the filter
Run, %IrfanViewPath% %InputFolder%\*.jpg %ConversionParams%
Run, %IrfanViewPath% %OutputFolder%\*.jpg %FilterParams%

Modify the InputFolder and OutputFolder variables to match the location of your input and output folders.
Save the file with a .ahk extension, for example batch_editing.ahk.
Double-click on the script file to run it.
The script will launch IrfanView and convert all the JPEG images in the input folder to the output folder, with a resolution of 1280x960 and a JPEG quality of 90. It will then apply a custom filter to the output images to enhance their colors.
Note that this is just a simple example, and there are many more ways to customize the script to your needs. For example, you can add more parameters to the ConversionParams and FilterParams variables to adjust the output format, resolution, and color balance. You can also use IrfanView's built-in batch processing tools to apply more complex filters and transformations to the images.<#sep#>-

def main():
    import sys
    input = sys.stdin.read().split()
    ptr = 0
    N = int(input[ptr])
    ptr += 1
    Q = int(input[ptr])
    ptr += 1
    a = list(map(int, input[ptr:ptr+N]))
    ptr += N
    queries = list(map(int, input[ptr:ptr+Q]))
    
    prev = {}
    next_ = {}
    for i in range(N):
        if i == 0:
            prev[a[i]] = None
        else:
            prev[a[i]] = a[i-1]
        if i == N-1:
            next_[a[i]] = None
        else:
            next_[a[i]] = a[i+1]
    
    head = a[0]
    tail = a[-1]
    
    for q in queries:
        current_prev = prev.get(q)
        current_next = next_.get(q)
        
        if current_next is not None:
            new_head = current_next
            prev[q] = tail
            next_[tail] = q
        else:
            new_head = q
            prev[q] = None
        
        if current_prev is not None:
            next_[q] = head
            prev[head] = q
            new_tail = current_prev
        else:
            next_[q] = None
            new_tail = q
        
        head = new_head
        tail = new_tail
    
    result = []
    current = head
    while current is not None:
        result.append(str(current))
        current = next_.get(current)
    print(' '.join(result))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()<#sep#> calculate how much it increased by. I’ll subtract 10.3 million from 12.0 million.

There are n positive integers a_1, a_2, …, a_n on Bob's homework paper, where n is always an even number. Bob is asked to divide those numbers into groups, where each group must contain at least 2 numbers. Suppose the numbers are divided into m groups, and the sum of the numbers in the j-th group is s_j. Bob's aim is to minimize the sum of the square of s_j, that is $$$∑_{j = 1}^{m} s_j^2.$$$

Bob is puzzled by this hard problem. Could you please help him solve it?
Input

The first line contains an even integer n (2 ≤ n ≤ 3 ⋅ 10^5), denoting that there are n integers on Bob's homework paper.

The second line contains n integers a_1, a_2, …, a_n (1 ≤ a_i ≤ 10^4), describing the numbers you need to deal with.

Output

A single line containing one integer, denoting the minimum of the sum of the square of s_j, which is $$$∑_{i = j}^{m} s_j^2, where m$$$ is the number of groups.

Examples

Input


4
8 5 2 3


Output


164


Input


6
1 1 1 2 2 2


Output


27

Note

In the first sample, one of the optimal solutions is to divide those 4 numbers into 2 groups \{2, 8\}, \{5, 3\}. Thus the answer is (2 + 8)^2 + (5 + 3)^2 = 164.

In the second sample, one of the optimal solutions is to divide those 6 numbers into 3 groups \{1, 2\}, \{1, 2\}, \{1, 2\}. Thus the answer is (1 + 2)^2 + (1 + 2)^2 + (1 + 2)^2 = 27.

+        return NumberExpression(KQL(f'array_index_of({to_kql(array)}, {to_kql(value)})'))

<#sep#>user: Как создать собственный искусственный язык?

assistant: Создание собственного искусственного языка - это интересный творческий процесс, который может включать в себя создание новых грамматических правил, слов, фонетики и лексики. Вот несколько шагов, которые могут помочь вам начать создание своего искусственного языка:

1.	Определите цель вашего языка. Зачем вы хотите создать новый язык? Будет ли он использоваться для литературных целей, в научных исследованиях или для игр?

2.	Решите, какую фонетику вы хотите использовать. Какие звуки будут входить в ваш язык, как они будут образовываться и как они будут записываться?

3.	Разработайте грамматику. Решите, как будут образовываться слова, какие грамматические категории будут использоваться, как будет происходить склонение и спряжение.

4.	Создайте базовый словарь. Определите, какие слова будут использоваться в вашем языке, и создайте словарь для этих слов.

5.	Разработайте свойства языка. Решите, какой будет порядок слов в предложении, как будут образовываться вопросы и отрицания, и какие будут использоваться связки.

6.	Проверьте свой язык. Протестируйте свой язык, чтобы убедиться, что он логичный и понятный. Вы можете использовать его для написания текстов на своём языке, чтобы проверить его практическую работоспособность.

7.	Задокументируйте свой язык. Создайте грамматический справочник и словарь, чтобы другие люди могли узнать о вашем языке.

Создание искусственного языка - это увлекательное занятие, и в нём могут быть свои сложности. Однако, если вы увлечены языками и готовы вложить в это время и усилия, то создание своего искусственного языка может быть очень увлекательным занятием.

user: Рассмотрим эльфийский язык (синдарин) и шифр Цезаря. Почему первый является искусственным языком, а второй - нет?

assistant: Синдарин является искусственным языком, так как он был создан толкиеном в качестве части мира Средиземья, описанного в его произведениях. Синдарин был разработан для использования в литературных целях и был доработан в течение многих лет, чтобы создать полноценный язык со своей грамматикой, лексикой и фонетической системой.

С другой стороны, шифр Цезаря не является языком, и он не создавался с целью коммуникации. Это простой метод шифрования, в котором каждая буква заменяется другой буквой, сдвинутой на определенное количество позиций в алфавите. Шифр Цезаря может использоваться для защиты сообщений, но он не обладает грамматикой, лексикой или другими характеристиками языка, необходимыми для коммуникации.

Таким образом, синдарин является полноценным языком, разработанным для коммуникации, а шифр Цезаря - простым методом шифрования, который не обладает характеристиками языка.<#sep#>-

n = int(input())
cards = []
for i in range(n):
    suit, num = input().split()
    cards.append((int(num), suit, i))

def partition(A, p, r):
    x = A[r]
    i = p - 1
    for j in range(p, r):
        if A[j][0] <= x[0]:
            i += 1
            A[i], A[j] = A[j], A[i]
    A[i+1], A[r] = A[r], A[i+1]
    return i + 1

def quicksort(A, p, r):
    if p < r:
        q = partition(A, p, r)
        quicksort(A, p, q-1)
        quicksort(A, q+1, r)

quicksort(cards, 0, n-1)

is_stable = True
for i in range(1, n):
    if cards[i][0] == cards[i-1][0] and cards[i][2] < cards[i-1][2]:
        is_stable = False
        break

print("Stable" if is_stable else "Not stable")
for card in cards:
    print(f"{card[1]} {card[0]}")<#sep#>Please answer the given financial question based on the context.

Alternatively, maybe construct a number S such that S can be written as (x+1)^2 + y1^2, (x+2)^2 + y2^2, ..., (x +n)^2 + y_n^2.

Let’s assume such S exists for some n. Then, for each i from 1 to n:

S - (x +i)^2 = y_i^2

So each S - (x +i)^2 must be a perfect square. Therefore, each y_i = sqrt(S - (x +i)^2) must be integer.

So, for each i, S must be congruent to a square modulo 1 (which it is), but more importantly, the differences between S and the squares of consecutive integers must themselves be squares.

Alternatively, the differences between consecutive terms:

For i and i+1:

(y_{i+1})^2 - (y_i)^2 = [S - (x + i +1)^2] - [S - (x + i)^2] = (x + i)^2 - (x +i +1)^2 = - (2x + 2i +1)

So, similar to earlier, we have:

y_{i+1}^2 - y_i^2 = - (2x + 2i +1 )

Which is the same equation as before.

Therefore, this leads us back to the same recurrence relation.

So, this is a system of n-1 equations:

For each 1 ≤ i ≤ n-1:

y_{i+1}^2 - y_i^2 = - (2x + 2i +1 )

Which can be rewritten as:

(y_{i+1} - y_i)(y_{i+1} + y_i) = - (2x + 2i +1 )

Each of these equations must hold for integer y_i, y_{i+1}, x.

Since each right-hand side is an odd negative integer, and the left-hand side is the product of two integers whose sum and difference are both even (since y_{i+1} and y_i are integers), we can deduce that both factors on the left must be odd, as we saw before.

Therefore, for each i, we can write:

y_{i+1} - y_i = d_i

y_{i+1} + y_i = e_i

Where d_i and e_i are integers such that d_i * e_i = - (2x + 2i +1 )

Moreover, since d_i and e_i are both odd, their product is odd, which matches the RHS. Also, since y_{i+1} = (d_i + e_i)/2 and y_i = (e_i - d_i)/2, we need (d_i + e_i) and (e_i - d_i) to be even, which they are because d_i and e_i are both odd.

Therefore, each pair (d_i, e_i) must be a pair of odd integers whose product is - (2x + 2i +1 ). Thus, for each i, we can choose different factor pairs, but given that x is fixed, all these equations must be satisfied simultaneously.

Therefore, this seems like a system that can be solved by choosing appropriate d_i and e_i for each i, but with the complication that the same x appears in each equation. So, the challenge is to find an x and a set of d_i, e_i that satisfy all these equations.

import bisect

def main():
    import sys
    input = sys.stdin.read
    data = input().split()
    idx = 0
    T = int(data[idx])
    idx += 1
    for _ in range(T):
        N, K = int(data[idx]), int(data[idx+1])
        idx +=2
        arr = list(map(int, data[idx:idx+N]))
        idx +=N
        arr.sort()
        min_diff = float('inf')
        i = 0
        j = N -1
        while i < j:
            current_sum = arr[i] + arr[j]
            current_diff = abs(current_sum - K)
            if current_diff < min_diff:
                min_diff = current_diff
            if current_sum < K:
                i +=1
            else:
                j -=1
        count =0
        for i in range(N):
            target1 = (K - min_diff) - arr[i]
            target2 = (K + min_diff) - arr[i]
            left = i +1
            right = N
            if target1 == target2:
                l = bisect.bisect_left(arr, target1, left, right)
                r = bisect.bisect_right(arr, target1, left, right)
                count += (r - l)
            else:
                l = bisect.bisect_left(arr, target1, left, right)
                r = bisect.bisect_right(arr, target1, left, right)
                count += (r - l)
                l = bisect.bisect_left(arr, target2, left, right)
                r = bisect.bisect_right(arr, target2, left, right)
                count += (r - l)
        print(min_diff, count)

**Final Approach**
The optimal way to minimize the sum of the squares of the group sums is to sort the array and pair the smallest remaining element with the largest remaining element. This ensures that the sums of the pairs are as balanced as possible, leading to the minimal total sum of squares. By iterating through the sorted array and pairing elements from both ends towards the center, we can efficiently compute the minimal sum.

### Steps:
1. **Sort the Array**: Begin by sorting the list of numbers in non-decreasing order.
2. **Pair Elements**: Pair the smallest and largest elements, moving inward. This is done by iterating from the start and end of the sorted array towards the center.
3. **Compute Sum of Squares**: For each pair, compute the square of their sum and accumulate this value as the total.

### Solution Code
```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))
a.sort()
sum_sq = 0
for i in range(n // 2):
    sum_sq += (a[i] + a[n - 1 - i]) ** 2
print(sum_sq)
```

### Explanation:
- **Sorting**: Sorting ensures that we can easily pair the smallest remaining element with the largest, which helps in balancing the sums and minimizing their squares due to the convex nature of the square function.
- **Pairing Strategy**: By pairing the first element (smallest) with the last (largest), the second with the second last, etc., we ensure that each pair's sum is as small as possible. This strategy results in the minimal possible sum of squares because the product of the extremes helps in reducing the variance of the sums.
- **Efficiency**: Sorting the array takes O(n log n) time, and iterating through the array to form pairs is O(n), making the solution efficient enough for the given constraints (up to 3e5 elements).
</think>
