 Thus in this scenario, row0 chosen by maximin, opponent can choose row2's move (j=2), leading to your payoff of grid[0][2] =9. 

 Thus the problem is that the maximin code, in this scenario where all of the opponent's options give them same value, so they can choose any j. But the code will choose the first max encountered (for their j), leading to possibly not the worst case.

 Thus the maximin approach is not conservative enough when there are ties in opponent's options. 

 Ah, so the toddler logic is flawed here.

When multiple j's give the same maximized payoff ( to the opponent ), then opponent could choose the one that is worse for you. 

Thus the proper way to account for it is:

 when the opponent has multiple choices that all give the same for their payoff, then the opponent could choose the one that is worst for us (i.e., gives us the lowest possible value among those).

 Hence, for the maximin calculation, when the opponent can choose between some j's for the maximal payoff, you have to consider the minimal value among the grid[i][j] for all j in that maximal set.

 Thus the code needs to compute all j's that gives the maximal grid[j][i], and then among THOSE J's, your minimal gain, and then choose the row with the maximum among those.

Wow, that's a painful nuance.

Thus the correct way to compute maximin:

For each i:

1. find all j's where grid[j][i] is maximum (i.e., the opponent's best payoff possible for when you pick row i)

2. among all those j's, your payoff is grid[i][j]. the minimal of those (worst possible for you, assuming the opponent would choose the worst possible j)\

3 then, the maximin value is that minimal payoff. choose the row with highest such value.

 Oh that's a key insight.

 Thus, the code must be adjusted accordingly.

 So in that example above where row0 is a good choice but in case of ties.

 The example was when all grid[j][i] (i=0) are zero's:

 the set of j's for maximum grid[j][i} is all j's. 

 Your payoff over those j's is grid[0][j], so the minimal among those is grid[0][0], grid[0][1}, grid[0][2}, whatever. 

 If your row has a 9 in column2, then that set includes 0,0,9 → minimal is0, but there exists a j (j=2) where you get 9 but the opponent is just as satisfied.

 However, the opponent could choose that j even though it gives same for them but bad for you.

 Hence, To compute the worst case assuming the opponent acts against you, even though it doesn't affect their pay, then in that case, they could choose any j (the ones with maximum), but bad for you.

 Thus the maximin's calculation must choose the minimal possible for you, i.e., among all j's that are maximally good for them (their grid[j][i] is highest), compute my payoff (grid[i][j]) for all those j， take the minimum of那些. that is the最坏scenario.

 thus, the correct code would:

 in the loop for i in each row:

find all the j's which give grid[j][i] the maximum value( the opponent's pay for选择j is maximum)

 then among those j's， find the minimum of grid[i][j], since the opponent could choose any of those j's, the worst case for me is the最小 of那些.

 thus the code should be adjusted:

instead of choosing the first max, need to compute all j's with max value and then min in my payoffs for those j's.

 this is a crucial adjustment.

 thus the updated code:

+                raise ValueError(f"Invalid value for parameter {key}={value:~}")
+
     def parameters(self):
         """Return groove parameters as dictionary of quantities."""
         return {k: v for k, v in self.__dict__.items() if isinstance(v, pint.Quantity)}

<#sep#>user: ¿ Podrías explicarme que es un lenguaje de programación orientado a objetos, las diferencias que tiene con respecto a un lenguaje de programación que no lo sea, decirme cual fue la necesidad que llevo a crear los lenguajes de programación orientados a objetos y darme una lista de los 10 mas usados ?

assistant: Claro, un lenguaje de programación orientado a objetos es un tipo de lenguaje de programación que se enfoca en la programación mediante el uso de objetos. En este campo, un objeto es una entidad que combina datos y comportamiento, y puede ser visto como una instancia de una clase.

En la programación orientada a objetos, se utiliza un enfoque basado en objetos para diseñar aplicaciones y programas, creando clases que describen objetos y luego creando objetos que pertenecen a esas clases. Cada objeto tiene su propio estado y comportamiento, y se puede enviar mensajes a otros objetos para que realicen alguna acción o para acceder a su información.

En comparación con los lenguajes de programación que no son orientados a objetos, los lenguajes de programación orientados a objetos tienen una serie de diferencias clave, incluyendo:

1) Abstracción: La programación orientada a objetos permite a los programadores abstraer los detalles de implementación y enfocarse en los objetos y sus relaciones.

2) Encapsulamiento: La programación orientada a objetos permite ocultar la información y el comportamiento de los objetos detrás de una interfaz pública, lo que aumenta la seguridad y reduce la complejidad del código.

3) Reutilización de código: La programación orientada a objetos permite la reutilización de código mediante la creación de clases que pueden ser utilizadas en múltiples objetos.

4) Modificación y escalabilidad: La programación orientada a objetos permite a los programadores modificar y escalar fácilmente el código, ya que los cambios en una clase se aplican a todos los objetos de esa clase.

En resumen, la programación orientada a objetos es un enfoque de programación que permite una mayor abstracción, encapsulamiento, reutilización de código y escalabilidad en comparación con los lenguajes de programación que no son orientados a objetos.


La necesidad de crear lenguajes de programación orientados a objetos surgió a medida que los programas y aplicaciones se volvían cada vez más complejos y difíciles de mantener y escalar. Los primeros lenguajes de programación eran estructurados y enfocados en un enfoque de programación basado en procedimientos, lo que significaba que los programas se dividían en procedimientos y funciones separados que trabajaban juntos para realizar tareas.

Sin embargo, a medida que los programas se volvían más grandes y complejos, se hizo cada vez más difícil mantener el control sobre el estado y los datos que fluyen a través de los programas. Además, el enfoque basado en procedimientos hacía que fuera difícil reutilizar el código y escalar el programa a medida que las necesidades de la aplicación cambiaban.

Por estas razones, surgió la necesidad de un nuevo enfoque de programación que permitiera una mayor abstracción y encapsulamiento de los datos y comportamientos, así como una mayor capacidad de reutilización y escalabilidad del código. Esto llevó a la creación de la programación orientada a objetos, que permitió a los programadores trabajar con objetos en lugar de procedimientos y funciones separados, lo que resultó en una programación más eficiente y mantenible.

def strategies_maximin(grid, store):

    best_val = -1

    best_row = -1

    for i in range(3):

        # compute all j's where grid[j][i] is maximized

        max_j_val = max( [ grid[j][i] for j in range(3) ] )

        possible_j = [j for j in range(3) if grid[j][i]==max_j_val]

        # among these j's, find the minimal grid[i][j]

        payoff_min = min( [grid[i][j] for j in possible_j] )

        # the maximin value for row i is this payout_min

        current_maximin = payoff_min

        # compare 

        if current_maximin > best_val or (current_maximin == best_val and i < best_row):

            best_val = current_maximin

            best_row = i

    return best_row

To tackle this problem, we need to create a bot for a 3-choice variant of the Prisoner's Dilemma where each round uses a randomly generated 3x3 payoff matrix. The goal is to maximize the bot's total score over 100 rounds by strategically choosing rows based on the current payoff matrix while adapting to the opponent's potential strategies.

### Approach
The strategy chosen here is the **maximin approach**, which is a game theory concept aimed at minimizing the worst-case scenario. Here's the detailed breakdown:

1. **Maximin Strategy**:
   - For each possible row choice (`i`), the bot calculates the opponent's best possible response. This is done by finding the column (`j`) that maximizes the opponent's payoff (`grid[j][i]`).
   - Among all columns that are optimal for the opponent, the bot considers the minimum payoff the bot could receive (`grid[i][j]` for each such `j`). This accounts for the opponent possibly choosing a column that, while best for them, is worst for the bot.
   - The bot then selects the row that maximizes this minimum payoff, ensuring the highest possible score in the worst-case scenario.

### Solution Code
```python
def maximin_strategize(grid: list[list[int]], store: dict) -> int:
    best_maximin = -1
    best_row = 0
    for i in range(3):
        # Find maximum value in column i (opponent's payoff for their row j choosing)
        max_val = max(grid[j][i] for j in range(3))
        # Collect all j that give this maximum value
        possible_j = [j for j in range(3) if grid[j][i] == max_val]
        # The worst-case payoff for this row (minimum of bot's payoff in these j)
        current_min = min(grid[i][j] for j in possible_j)
        if current_min > best_maximin:
            best_maximin = current_min
            best_row = i
        elif current_min == best_maximin and i < best_row:
            best_row = i  # break ties by choosing the smallest index
    return best_row

def maximin_interpret(grid: list[list[int]], moves: tuple[int, int], store: dict) -> None:
    opp_move = moves[1]
    if 'opp_counts' not in store:
        store['opp_counts'] = [0, 0, 0]
    store['opp_counts'][opp_move] += 1
```

### Explanation
- **strategize Function**:
  - **Loop Through Each Row**: For each row `i`, determine the opponent's best responses by finding columns `j` that maximize their payoff (`grid[j][i]`).
  - **Calculate Worst-Case Payoff**: Among all optimal columns for the opponent, compute the minimum payoff the bot could receive (`grid[i][j]` for those columns).
  - **Choose Best Row**: Select the row that maximizes this minimum payoff, ensuring the highest score even in the worst-case scenario where the opponent plays optimally against the bot.

- **interpret Function**:
  - Track the opponent's move history for potential future use (though this implementation focuses solely on the maximin strategy).

This approach ensures the bot makes robust decisions by always preparing for the opponent's optimal play, thus minimizing the risk of low scores even when facing unpredictable opponents. The solution leverages game theory to balance between strategic aggressiveness and cautious conservatism, making it effective against various opponent strategies.
